Separating Sessions Smoothly
Simon Fowler ORCID:0000-0001-5143-5475, Wen Kokke ORCID:0000-0002-1360-4714, Ornela Dardha ORCID:0000-0001-9927-7875, Sam Lindley ORCID:0000-0002-1360-4714, J. Garrett Morris ORCID:0000-0002-3992-1080

Abstract
. This paper introduces Hypersequent GV (HGV), a modular and extensible core calculus for functional programming with session types that enjoys deadlock freedom, confluence, and strong normalisation. HGV exploits hyper-environments, which are collections of type environments, to ensure that structural congruence is type preserving. As a consequence we obtain an operational correspondence between HGV and HCP—a process calculus based on hypersequents and in a propositions-as-types correspondence with classical linear logic (CLL). Our translations from HGV to HCP and vice-versa both preserve and reflect reduction. HGV scales smoothly to support Girard’s Mix rule, a crucial ingredient for channel forwarding and exceptions.

1.
Introduction

Session types
[
Hon93
,
THK94
,
HVK98
]
are types used to
model and verify communication protocols in concurrent and distributed systems:
just as data types rule out dividing an integer by a string, session
types rule out sending an unexpected message. Session types
originated in process calculi, but there is a gap between process
calculi, which model the evolving state of concurrent systems, and the
descriptions of these systems in mainstream programming languages. This
paper addresses two foundations for session types: (1) a session-typed
concurrent lambda calculus called GV
[
LM15
]
,
intended to be a modular and extensible basis for functional
programming languages with session types; and, (2) a session-typed
process calculus called CP
[
Wad14
]
, with a
propositions-as-types correspondence to classical linear logic
(CLL)
[
Gir87
]
.

Processes in CP correspond exactly to proofs in CLL and deadlock
freedom follows from cut-elimination for CLL. However, while CP is
strongly tied to CLL, at the same time it departs from the

\pi
-calculus. Independent
\pi
-calculus features can only appear in
combination in CP: CP combines name restriction with parallel
composition (
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu x)(P\parallel Q)}}
), corresponding to CLL’s cut
rule, and combines sending (of bound names only) with parallel
composition (
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[y].(P\parallel Q)}}
), corresponding to CLL’s
tensor rule. This results in a proliferation of process constructors
and prevents the use of standard techniques from concurrency theory,
such as labelled-transition semantics and bisimulation, since the expected
transitions give rise to ill-typed terms. For example, we cannot write the expected transition
rule for output,

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[y].(P\parallel Q)}}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[y]}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel Q}
,
since
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel Q}
is not a valid CP process. A similar issue
arises when attempting to design a synchronisation transition rule for bound
output (see
[
KMP19b
]
for a detailed discussion).
Inspired by Carbone
et al.

[
CMS18
]
who use
hypersequents
[
Avr91
]
to give a logical grounding to
choreographic programming languages
[
Mon13
]
,
Hypersequent CP
(HCP)
[
KMP19a
,
KMP19b
,
MP18
]
restores the
independence of these features by factoring out parallel composition
into a standalone construct while retaining the close correspondence
with CLL proofs. HCP typing reasons about collections of processes
using collections of type environments (or
hyper-environments
).

GV extends linear
\lambda
-calculus with constants for session-typed
communication. Following Gay and Vasconcelos
[
GV10
]
,
Lindley and Morris
[
LM15
]
describe GV’s semantics
by combining a reduction relation on single terms, following standard

\lambda
-calculus rules, and a reduction relation on concurrent
configurations of terms, following standard
\pi
-calculus rules.
They give a semantic characterisation of deadlocked processes, an
extrinsic
[
Rey00
]
type system for configurations, and show
that well-typed configurations are deadlock-free. There is, however,
a large fly in this otherwise smooth ointment: GV’s process equivalence
does not preserve typing. As a result, it is not enough for Lindley
and Morris to show progress and preservation for well-typed
configurations; instead, they must show progress and preservation for

all
configurations
equivalent to
well-typed
configurations. This not only complicates the metatheory of GV, but
the burden is inherited by any effort to build on GV’s account of
concurrency
[
FLMD19
]
.

In this paper, we show that using hyper-environments in the typing of
configurations enables a metatheory for GV that, compared to that of
Lindley and Morris, is simpler, is more general, and as a result is
easier to use and easier to extend. Hypersequent GV (HGV) repairs the
treatment of process equivalence—equivalent configurations are
equivalently typeable—and avoids the need for formal gimmickry
connecting name restriction and parallel composition. HGV admits
standard semantic techniques for concurrent programs: we use
bisimulation to show that our translations both preserve
and
reflect
reduction, whereas Lindley and Morris resort to weak explicit
substitutions
[
LM99
]
and only show that their translations between GV
and CP preserve reduction.
HGV is also more easily
extensible: we outline three examples, including showing that HGV
naturally extends to disconnected sets of communication processes,
without any change to the proof of deadlock freedom, and that it
serves as a simpler foundation for existing work on exceptions in
GV
[
FLMD19
]
.

Contributions

The paper contributes the following:

•

Section

3
introduces Hypersequent GV (HGV), a modular and
extensible core calculus for functional programming with session
types which uses hyper-environments to ensure that structural
congruence is type preserving.

•

Section

4
shows that every well-typed GV
configuration is also a well-typed HGV configuration, and every
tree-structured HGV configuration is equivalent to a well-typed GV
configuration.

•

Section

5
gives an operational
correspondences between HGV and HCP via translations in both
directions that preserve and reflect reduction.

•

Section

6
demonstrates the extensibility of HGV
through: (1) unconnected processes, (2) a simplified treatment of
forwarding, and (3) an improved foundation for exceptions.

Section

2
reviews GV and its metatheory,

Section

7
discusses why it is difficult to apply hyper-environments to
term typing,

Section

8
discusses related work, and

Section

9
concludes and discusses future work.

This paper is an improved and extended version of a paper published at
CONCUR 2021
[
FKD+21
]
. Additional highlights include:

•

a more detailed account of process structures;

•

a more detailed account of extensions;

•

a more detailed account of the metatheory for HCP; and

•

a modified formulation of HCP’s labelled transition system and the
translation of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\mathbf{fork}}}
in Section
5

fixing errors in the operational correspondence result from the
CONCUR 2021 paper.

Proofs of all of the technical results are included in the paper.

2.
The Equivalence Embroglio

GV programs are deadlock free, which GV ensures by restricting process
structures to trees. A
process structure
is an undirected
graph where nodes represent processes and edges represent channels
shared between the connected nodes. Session-typed programs with an
acyclic process structure are deadlock-free by construction.
We illustrate this with a session-typed vending machine example
written in GV.

{exa}

Consider the session type of a vending machine below, which sells
chocolate bars and lollipops. If the vending machine is free, the
customer can press
\raisebox{-.9pt} {1}⃝
to receive a chocolate bar or
\raisebox{-.9pt} {2}⃝
to
receive a lollipop. If the vending machine is busy, the session
ends.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathsf{VendingMachine}}

\displaystyle\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\oplus}\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathsf{Free}&:&{\&}\left\{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {1}}}\;{:}\;!{\mathsf{ChocolateBar}}.{\mathbf{end}_{!}},\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {2}}}\;{:}\;!{\mathsf{Lollipop}}.{\mathbf{end}_{!}}\right\}\\
\mathsf{Busy}&:&{\mathbf{end}_{!}}\end{array}\right\}}

The customer’s session type is
dual
: where the vending machine sends a
\mathsf{ChocolateBar}
, the customer receives a
\mathsf{ChocolateBar}
, and so forth.
Figure

1
shows the vending machine and customer as a GV program with its process structure.

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbf{let}\;\mathsf{vendingMachine}=\lambda{s}.\\
\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbf{let}\;s=\mathbf{select}\;\mathsf{Free}\;{s}\;\mathbf{in}\\
\mathbf{let}\;{s}=\mathbf{offer}\;{s}\;\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {1}}}&\mapsto&\mathbf{send}\;\mathsf{chocolateBar}\\
\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {2}}}&\mapsto&\mathbf{send}\;\mathsf{lollipop}\end{array}\right\}\\
\mathbf{close}\;{s}\end{array}}\\
\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{let}\;\mathsf{customer}=\lambda{s}.\\
\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbf{offer}\;{s}\;\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathsf{Free}&\mapsto&\mathbf{let}\;s=\mathbf{select}\;\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {1}}}\;{s}\;\mathbf{in}\\
&&\mathbf{let}\;(\mathsf{cb},s)=\mathbf{recv}\;{s}\;\mathbf{in}\\
&&\mathbf{wait}\;{s};\mathbf{eat}\;cb\\
\mathsf{Busy}&\mapsto&\mathbf{wait}\;{s};\mathbf{hungry}\end{array}\right\}\end{array}}\\
\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{let}\;{s}={\mathbf{fork}\;(\lambda{s}.\mathsf{vendingMachine}\;{s})}\\
\mathbf{in}\;\mathsf{customer}\;{s}\end{array}}

(a)
Vending machine and customer as a GV program.

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathsf{vendingMachine}}
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathsf{customer}}
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}s}
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}s}

(b)
Process structure of
Figure

1
.

Figure 1.
Example program with acyclic process structure.

GV establishes the restriction to tree-structured processes by restricting the primitive for spawning processes. In GV,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{fork}}}
has type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{({S}\mathbin{\multimap}{\mathbf{end}_{!}})}\mathbin{\multimap}{\overline{S}}}}
. It takes a closure of type
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{S}\mathbin{\multimap}{\mathbf{end}_{!}}}}
as an argument, creates a channel with endpoints of dual types
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}}}
, spawns the closure as a new process by supplying one of the endpoints as an argument, and then returns the other endpoint. In essence,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{fork}}}
is a branching operation on the process structure: it creates a new node connected to the current node by a single edge. Linearity guarantees that the tree structure is preserved, even in the presence of higher-order channels.

Lindley and Morris
[
LM15
]
introduce a semantics for GV, which evaluates programs embedded in process configurations, consisting of embedded programs, flagged as main (
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet}}
) or child (
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ}}
) threads,
\nu
-binders to create new channels, and parallel compositions:

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}\Coloneqq{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet\;M}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\;M}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu x)\mathcal{C}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\parallel}\mathcal{D})}

They introduce these process configurations together with a standard structural congruence, which allows, amongst other things, the reordering of processes using commutativity (
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\parallel}\mathcal{C^{\prime}}}\equiv{\mathcal{C^{\prime}}\mathbin{\parallel}\mathcal{C}}}}
), associativity (
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\parallel}(\mathcal{C^{\prime}}\mathbin{\parallel}\mathcal{C^{\prime\prime}})}\equiv{(\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\parallel}\mathcal{C^{\prime}})\mathbin{\parallel}\mathcal{C^{\prime\prime}}}}}
), and scope extrusion
(
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\parallel}(\nu x)\mathcal{C^{\prime}}}\equiv{(\nu x)(\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\parallel}\mathcal{C^{\prime}})}}}
if
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}\notin\fv({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}})}
). They guarantee acyclicity by defining an extrinsic type system for configurations. In particular, the type system requires that in every parallel composition
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}\mathbin{\parallel}\mathcal{D}}}
, configurations

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}}
must have exactly one channel in common, and that in a name restriction
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu x)\mathcal{C}}}
, channel
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
cannot be used until it is shared across a parallel composition.

These restrictions are sufficient to guarantee deadlock freedom. Unfortunately, they are
not
preserved by process equivalence. As Lindley and Morris write,
(noting that their name restrictions bind
channels
rather than endpoint pairs, and their
(\nu xy)
abbreviates
(\nu x)(\nu y)
):

Alas, our notion of typing is not preserved by configuration equivalence. For example, assume that
\Gamma\vdash(\nu xy)(C_{1}\parallel(C_{2}\parallel C_{3}))
, where
x\in\fv(C_{1}),y\in\fv(C_{2}),\text{ and }x,y\in\fv(C_{3})
. We have that
C_{1}\parallel(C_{2}\parallel C_{3})\equiv(C_{1}\parallel C_{2})\parallel C_{3}
, but
\Gamma\nvdash(\nu xy)((C_{1}\parallel C_{2})\parallel C_{3})
, as both
x
and
y
must be shared between the processes
C_{1}\parallel C_{2}
and
C_{3}
.

As a result, standard notions of progress and preservation are not enough to guarantee deadlock freedom, as reduction sequences could include equivalence steps from well-typed to non-well-typed terms. Instead, they must prove a stronger result:

Theorem 3

(Lindley and Morris
[
LM15
]
)
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C^{\prime}}}}
, and
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C^{\prime}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D^{\prime}}}}
, then there exists
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}}
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D^{\prime}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}}
.

This is not a one-time cost: languages based on GV must either also give up on type preservation for structural congruence
[
FLMD19
]
or admit deadlocks
[
ITT+19
,
TV20
]
.

Note that CP only avoids the same issue through its combined
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu x)(P\parallel Q)}
term; attempts to split the term into a separate name restriction and parallel composition would also lose typability of equivalence.

3.
Hypersequent GV

Typing rules for terms

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}

9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TM-Pair}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\times}{T^{\prime}}}\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T^{\prime}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;(x,y)=M\;\mathbf{in}\;N}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TM-LetPair}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{0}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{absurd}\;M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TM-Absurd}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inl}\;M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{+}{U}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TM-Inl}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inr}\;M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{+}{U}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TM-Inr}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{+}{T^{\prime}}}\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T^{\prime}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{case}\;L\left\{\mathbf{inl}\;x\mapsto{M};\;\mathbf{inr}\;y\mapsto{N}\right\}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TM-CaseSum}

Type schemas for communication primitives

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}K}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}

\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{{S}\mathbin{\times}{\overline{S}}}\mathbin{\multimap}{\mathbf{end}_{!}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{fork}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{({S}\mathbin{\multimap}{\mathbf{end}_{!}})}\mathbin{\multimap}{\overline{S}}}\\
\end{array}\par\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{send}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{{T}\mathbin{\times}{!{T}.{S}}}\mathbin{\multimap}{S}}\\

Duality

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{!{T}.{S}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?{T}.{\overline{S}}}\par{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{?{T}.{S}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!{T}.{\overline{S}}}\par{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{\mathbf{end}_{!}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{?}}\par{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{\mathbf{end}_{?}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{!}}

Figure 2.
HGV, duality and typing rules for terms.

We present Hypersequent GV (HGV), a linear
\lambda
-calculus extended
with session types and primitives for session-typed communication. HGV
shares its syntax and static typing with GV, but uses
hyper-environments for runtime typing to simplify and generalise its
semantics.

Types, terms, and static typing

Types (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}
) comprise a unit type (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}
), an
empty type (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{0}}
), product types (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\times}{U}}
), sum
types (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{+}{U}}
), linear function types
(
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\multimap}{U}}
), and session types (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}
).

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}U}\Coloneqq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{0}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\times}{U}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{+}{U}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{T}\mathbin{\multimap}{U}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}\par{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}\Coloneqq\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!{T}.{S}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?{T}.{S}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{!}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{?}}

Session types (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}
) comprise output (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!{T}.{S}}
: send a value of type

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
, then behave like
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}
), input (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?{T}.{S}}
: receive a value of type

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
, then behave like
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}
), and dual end types (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{!}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{?}}
).
The dual endpoints restrict process structure to

trees

[
Wad14
]
; conflating them loosens this
restriction to
forests

[
ALM16
]
.
We let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}
range over type environments.

The terms and typing rules are given
in
Figure

2
. The linear
\lambda
-calculus rules
are standard; communication primitives
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}K}
are given as constants.
Each communication primitive
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}K}
has a type schema:
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}
takes a
pair of compatible endpoints and forwards all messages between them;

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{fork}}
takes a function, which is passed one endpoint (of type

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}
) of a fresh channel yielding a new child thread, and returns
the other endpoint (of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}}
);
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{send}}
takes a pair
of a value and an endpoint, sends the value over the endpoint, and
returns an updated endpoint;
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{recv}}
takes an endpoint, receives
a value over the endpoint, and returns the pair of the received value
and an updated endpoint; and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{wait}}
synchronises on a terminated
endpoint of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{?}}
.
Output is dual to input, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{!}}
is dual to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{?}}
.
Duality is involutive,
i
.
e
.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{\overline{S}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}
.

We write
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M;N}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;()=M\;\mathbf{in}\;N}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;x=M\;\mathbf{in}\;N}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\lambda x.N)\;M}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda().M}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda z.z;M}
, and

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda(x,y).M}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda z.\mathbf{let}\;(x,y)=z\;\mathbf{in}\;M}
.
We write
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}K}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
for
\cdot\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}K}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
in typing
derivations.

Remark 1
.

We include
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}
because it is convenient for the
correspondence with CP, which interprets CLL’s axiom as
forwarding. We
can
encode
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}
in GV via a type
directed translation akin to CLL’s
identity expansion
.

Typing rules for configurations

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TC-New}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{R}\sqcap{R^{\prime}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TC-Par}\\
\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet\;M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bullet\;T}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TC-Main}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{!}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\;M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\circ}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TC-Child}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{?}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x{\overset{z}{\leftrightarrow}}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\circ}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TC-Link}

Configuration types

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}\Coloneqq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\circ}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bullet\;T}

Configuration type combination

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}\sqcap{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R^{\prime}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bullet\;T}\sqcap{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\circ}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bullet\;T}\hskip 18.49988pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\circ}\sqcap\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bullet\;T}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bullet\;T}\hskip 18.49988pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\circ}\sqcap\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\circ}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\circ}

Figure 3.
HGV, typing rules for configurations.

Configurations and runtime typing

Process configurations (
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{E}}
)
comprise child threads (
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\;M}
), the main thread
(
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet\;M}
), link threads (
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x{\overset{z}{\leftrightarrow}}y}
), name
restrictions (
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)\mathcal{C}}
), and parallel compositions
(
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D}}
).
We refer to a configuration of the
form
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ M}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x{\overset{z}{\leftrightarrow}}y}
as an

auxiliary thread
, and a configuration of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet M}

as a
main thread
. We let
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{A}}
range over auxiliary
threads and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{T}}
range over all threads (auxiliary or main).

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi}\Coloneqq{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ}\qquad\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{E}}\Coloneqq{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi\;M}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x{\overset{z}{\leftrightarrow}}y}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)\mathcal{C}}

The configuration language is reminiscent of
\pi
-calculus processes,
but has some non-standard features. Name restriction uses double
binders
[
Vas12
]
in which one name is bound
to each endpoint of the channel. Link
threads
[
LM16
]
handle forwarding. A link thread

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x{\overset{z}{\leftrightarrow}}y}
waits for the thread connected to
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z}

to terminate before forwarding all messages between
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
.

Configuration typing departs from GV
[
LM15
]
,
exploiting
hypersequents

[
Avr91
]
to
recover modularity and extensibility. Inspired by
HCP
[
MP18
,
KMP19b
,
KMP19a
]
, configurations
are typed under a
hyper-environment
, an unordered collection of disjoint
type environments.
We let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}}
range over hyper-environments,
writing
\varnothing
for the empty hyper-environment,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}
for disjoint extension of

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}
with type environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}
, and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}
for disjoint concatenation of

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}
.

The typing rules for configurations are given
in
Figure

3
.
Rules
\LabTirName
TC-New and
\LabTirName
TC-Par are key to deadlock
freedom:
\LabTirName
TC-New joins two disjoint configurations with a
new channel, and merges their type environments;

\LabTirName
TC-Par combines two disjoint configurations, and registers
their disjointness by separating their type environments in the
hyper-environment.
Rules
\LabTirName
TC-Main,
\LabTirName
TC-Child, and
\LabTirName
TC-Link
type main, child, and link threads, respectively; all three require a
singleton hyper-environment.
A configuration has type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\circ}
if it has no main thread, and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bullet\;\;T}
if it has a main thread of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
.
The configuration type combination operator ensures that a well-typed
configuration has at most one main thread.

Values and evaluation contexts

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\text{Values}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}U},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}W}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}K}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda x.M}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}()}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(V,W)}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inl}\;V}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inr}\;V}\\
\text{Evaluation contexts}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\square}\\
&&\;\mid&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E\;M}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V\;E}\\
&&\;\mid&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;()=E\;\mathbf{in}\;M}\\
&&\;\mid&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(E,M)}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(V,E)}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;(x,y)=E\;\mathbf{in}\;M}\\
&&\;\mid&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inl}\;E}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inr}\;E}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{case}\;E\left\{\mathbf{inl}\;x\mapsto{M};\;\mathbf{inr}\;y\mapsto{N}\right\}}\\
\text{Thread contexts}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}F}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi\;E}\end{array}

Term reduction

M\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{M}}N

\begin{array}[]{llcl}\LabTirName{E-Lam}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\lambda x.M)\;V}&\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{M}}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M\{V/x\}}\\
\LabTirName{E-Unit}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;()=()\;\mathbf{in}\;M}&\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{M}}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}\\
\LabTirName{E-Pair}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;(x,y)=(V,W)\;\mathbf{in}\;M}&\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{M}}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M\{V/x,W/y\}}\\
\LabTirName{E-Inl}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{case}\;\mathbf{inl}\;V\left\{\mathbf{inl}\;x\mapsto{M};\;\mathbf{inr}\;y\mapsto{N}\right\}}&\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{M}}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M\{V/x\}}\\
\LabTirName{E-Inr}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{case}\;\mathbf{inr}\;V\left\{\mathbf{inl}\;x\mapsto{M};\;\mathbf{inr}\;y\mapsto{N}\right\}}&\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{M}}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N\{V/y\}}\\
\LabTirName{E-Lift}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E[M]}&\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{M}}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E[N]},\text{ if }{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{M}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}\end{array}

Structural congruence

\mathcal{C}\equiv\mathcal{D}

\begin{array}[t]{@{}c@{\quad}c@{}}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{\quad}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}\LabTirName{SC-ParAssoc}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}\parallel(\mathcal{D}\parallel\mathcal{E})}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D})\parallel\mathcal{E}}\\
\LabTirName{SC-NewComm}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)(\nu zw)\mathcal{C}}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu zw)(\nu xy)\mathcal{C}}\\
\LabTirName{SC-ScopeExt}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)(\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D})}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}\parallel(\nu xy)\mathcal{D}},\text{ if }{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}\notin\fv({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}})}\end{array}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{\quad}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}\LabTirName{SC-ParComm}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D}}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}\parallel\mathcal{C}}\\
\LabTirName{SC-NewSwap}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)\mathcal{C}}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu yx)\mathcal{C}}\\
\LabTirName{SC-LinkComm}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x{\overset{z}{\leftrightarrow}}y}&\equiv&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y{\overset{z}{\leftrightarrow}}x}\end{array}\end{array}

Configuration reduction

\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{llcl}\LabTirName{E-Reify-Fork}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}F[\mathbf{fork}\;{V}]}&\longrightarrow&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(F[x]\parallel\circ\;{(V\;x^{\prime})})},\text{ where }{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}\text{ fresh}\\
\LabTirName{E-Reify-Link}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}F[\mathbf{link}\;(x,y)]}&\longrightarrow&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu zz^{\prime})(x{\overset{z}{\leftrightarrow}}y\parallel F[z^{\prime}])},\text{ where }{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z^{\prime}}\text{ fresh}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{llcl}\LabTirName{E-Comm-Link}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu zz^{\prime})(\nu xx^{\prime})(x{\overset{z}{\leftrightarrow}}y\parallel\circ\;z^{\prime}\parallel\phi\;M)}&\longrightarrow&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi\;(M\{y/x^{\prime}\})}\\

\LabTirName{E-Comm-Close}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)(\circ\;y\parallel F[\mathbf{wait}\;{x}])}&\longrightarrow&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{F[()]}}\end{array}\\
\\
\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C^{\prime}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)\mathcal{C^{\prime}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=E-Res}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\parallel\mathcal{D}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=E-Par}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C^{\prime}}}\hskip 8.04996pt{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C^{\prime}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D^{\prime}}}\hskip 8.04996pt{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D^{\prime}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=E-Equiv}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{M}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}F[M]}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}F[M^{\prime}]}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=E-Lift-M}\end{array}

Figure 4.
HGV, operational semantics.

Operational semantics

Figure

4
gives the operational semantics for HGV, presented as a
deterministic reduction relation on terms and a nondeterministic
reduction relation on configurations.
HGV values (
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}U}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}W}
), evaluation contexts
(
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E}
), and term reduction rules (
\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{M}}
) define a standard
call-by-value, left-to-right evaluation strategy.
A closed term either reduces to a value or is blocked on a
communication action.

Thread contexts (
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}F}
) extend evaluation contexts to threads.
The structural congruence rules are standard apart from

\LabTirName
SC-LinkComm, which ensures links are undirected, and

\LabTirName
SC-NewSwap, which swaps names in double binders.

The configuration reduction relation gives a semantics for HGV’s communication and
concurrency constructs.
The first two rules,
\LabTirName
E-Reify-Fork and

\LabTirName
E-Reify-Link, create child and link threads, respectively.
The next three rules,
\LabTirName
E-Comm-Link,
\LabTirName
E-Comm-Send,
and
\LabTirName
E-Comm-Close perform communication actions.
The final four rules enable reduction under name restriction and
parallel composition, rewriting by structural congruence, and term
reduction in threads.
Two rules handle links:
\LabTirName
E-Reify-Link creates a new

link thread

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x{\overset{z}{\leftrightarrow}}y}
which blocks on
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z}
of
type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{?}}
, one endpoint of a fresh channel. The other
endpoint,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z^{\prime}}
of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{!}}
, is placed in the evaluation
context of the parent thread. When
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z^{\prime}}
terminates a child thread,

\LabTirName
E-Comm-Link performs forwarding by substitution.

Remark 2
.

Note that
\LabTirName
E-Comm-Link does not fire if
z^{\prime}
is returned by a main
thread.
In closed configurations, typing ensures that such a configuration
cannot arise: intuitively, a main thread can only obtain endpoints by

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{fork}}
or by receiving an endpoint.

Endpoints generated to communicate with forked
threads (i.e., those passed to a child thread) will always have a
session type terminating with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{?}}
, and a child thread cannot
transmit an endpoint ending in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{!}}
, since the endpoint must be
returned.
Consequently, there is no way for a main thread to obtain endpoints
with dual session types as required by the type of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}
. The case
for open configurations is accounted for by our open progress result
(see
Section

3.1
).

Choice

HGV does not include constructs for internal and external choice (for example,
as shown in the vending machine example in Section
1
).
Internal and external choice are instead encoded with sum types and session
delegation
[
Kob03
,
DGS17
]
.
Prior encodings of choice in GV
[
LM15
]
are
asynchronous. Instead, to encode synchronous choice we add a ‘dummy’
synchronisation before exchanging the value of sum type, as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{S}\mathbin{\oplus}{S^{\prime}}}&\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!{\mathbf{1}}.{!{({\overline{S_{1}}}\mathbin{+}{\overline{S_{2}}})}.{\mathbf{end}_{!}}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{S}\mathbin{\with}{S^{\prime}}}&\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?{\mathbf{1}}.{?{({S_{1}}\mathbin{+}{S_{2}})}.{\mathbf{end}_{?}}}}\\
\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\oplus}\{\}}&\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!{\mathbf{1}}.{!{\mathbf{0}}.{\mathbf{end}_{!}}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\with}\{\}}&\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?{\mathbf{1}}.{?{\mathbf{0}}.{\mathbf{end}_{?}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{select}\;\ell}&\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda{x}.\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbf{let}\;x=\mathbf{send}\;((),x)\;\mathbf{in}\\
\mathbf{fork}\;(\lambda y.\mathbf{send}\;(\ell\;y,x))\end{array}\right)}\\
\lx@intercol{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{offer}\;L\;\{\mathbf{inl}\;x\mapsto{M};\mathbf{inr}\;y\mapsto{N}\}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\triangleq&\!{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbf{let}\;((),z)=\mathbf{recv}\;L\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{let}\;(w,z)={\mathbf{recv}\;z}\\
\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{wait}\;z;\mathbf{case}\;w\left\{\mathbf{inl}\;x\mapsto{M};\;\mathbf{inr}\;y\mapsto{N}\right\}\end{array}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{offer}\;L\;\{\}}&\triangleq&\!{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbf{let}\;((),c)=\mathbf{recv}\;L\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{let}\;(z,c)={\mathbf{recv}\;c}\\
\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{wait}\,c;\mathbf{absurd}\;\,z\end{array}}\end{array}

3.1.
Metatheory

HGV enjoys type preservation, deadlock freedom, confluence, and strong
normalisation.

Preservation

Hyper-environments enable type preservation under structural
congruence, which significantly simplifies the metatheory compared to
GV.

Theorem 3

(Preservation)
.

(1)

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}
.

(2)

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}
, then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}
.

Proof 3.1
.

By induction on the derivations of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}
.
See
Appendix

A
.

Before moving onto progress, we must introduce some technical machinery to allow
us to reason about the structure of HGV programs.

Abstract process structures

Unlike in GV, in HGV we cannot rely on the fact that exactly one
channel is split over each parallel composition.
Instead, we introduce the notion of an
abstract process
structure
(APS).
Abstract process structures are a crucial ingredient in showing that HGV
configurations can be written in
tree canonical form
, which helps both
with establishing progress results and also the correspondence between HGV and
GV.

We begin by establishing the intuition behind the notion of an APS, and then
describe the formal definitions.
An APS is a graph defined over a hyper-environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}

and a set of undirected pairs of co-names (a
co-name set
)

\mathcal{N}
drawn from the names in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}
.

The nodes of an APS are the type environments in

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}
. Each edge is labelled by a distinct co-name pair

\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{2}}\}\in\mathcal{N}
, such that
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{1}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{2}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}}
.

{exa}

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{3}}
,
where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S_{2}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{3}}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S_{2}}}
, and suppose
\mathcal{N}=\{\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}\},\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}\}\}
.
The APS for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathcal{N}
is illustrated to the
right.

\Gamma_{1}
\Gamma_{2}
\Gamma_{3}
\{x,x^{\prime}\}
\{y,y^{\prime}\}
\{\{x,x^{\prime}\},\{y,y^{\prime}\}\}

{exa}

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{3}}
, where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S_{3}}}
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S_{2}}
,
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{3}}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S_{2}}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S_{3}}
,
and suppose
\mathcal{N}=\{\{x,x^{\prime}\},\{y,y^{\prime}\},\{z,z^{\prime}\}\}
.
The APS for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathcal{N}
is illustrated to the
right.

\Gamma_{1}
\Gamma_{2}
\Gamma_{3}
\{x,x^{\prime}\}
\{y,y^{\prime}\}
\{z,z^{\prime}\}
\{\{x,x^{\prime}\},\{y,y^{\prime}\},\{z,z^{\prime}\}\}

Let us now discuss the formal definition of an APS.
We begin by recalling the definition of an
undirected edge-labelled
multigraph
: an undirected graph that allows multiple edges between vertices.

{defi}

[Undirected Multigraph]
An
undirected multigraph

G
is a 3-tuple
(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},r)
where:

(1)

\mathcal{V}
is a set of vertices

(2)

\mathcal{E}
is a set of edge names

(3)

r
is a function
r:\mathcal{E}\mapsto\{\{v,w\}:v,w\in\mathcal{V}\}
from
edge names to an unordered pair of vertices

Denote the size of a set as
\absolutevalue{\cdot}
.
A
path
is a sequence of edges connecting two vertices. A multigraph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},r)
is
connected
if
\absolutevalue{\graphvar{V}}=1
, or if for every pair of vertices
v,w\in\mathcal{V}
there is a path between
v
and
w
. A multigraph is
acyclic
if no path forms a cycle.
A
leaf
is a vertex connected to the remainder of a graph by a
single edge.

{defi}

[Leaf]
Given an undirected multigraph
(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},r)
, a vertex
v\in\mathcal{V}
is a
leaf
if there exists a single
e\in\mathcal{E}

such that
v\in r(e)
.

In an undirected tree containing at least two vertices, there must be at least two leaves.

Lemma 4
.

If
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},r)
is an undirected tree where

\absolutevalue{V}\geq 2
, then there exist at least two leaves in

\mathcal{V}
.

Proof 3.2
.

For
G
to be an undirected tree where
\absolutevalue{V}\geq 2
and have fewer than
two leaves, then there would need to be a cycle, contradicting
acyclicity.

With the graph preliminaries in place, we are now ready to introduce the formal
definition of an APS.

{defi}

[Abstract process structure]
The
abstract process structure of a hyper-environment

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}

with respect to a co-name set

\mathcal{N}=\{\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y_{1}}\},\ldots,\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{n}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y_{n}}\}\}
is an undirected multigraph
(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},r)
defined as follows:

(1)

\mathcal{V}=\mathsf{envs}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}})

(2)

\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{N}

(3)

r=(\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}\}\mapsto\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}}\})
for each
\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}\}\in\mathcal{N}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}}\in\mathsf{envs}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}}\in\mathsf{envs}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}),{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}\in\fv({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}}),{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}\in\fv({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}})

{exa}

The formal definition of the APS described in Example
3.1
is defined as:

•

\mathcal{V}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{3}}\}

•

\mathcal{E}=\{\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}\},\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}\}\}

•

r(\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}\})\mapsto\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}}\})\\
r(\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}\})\mapsto\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{3}}\})

Whereas Example
3.1
is a tree,
Example
3.1
contains a cycle.
Only configurations typeable under a hyper-environment with a
tree
structure
can be written in tree canonical form.

{defi}

[Tree structure]
A hyper-environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}
with co-name set
\mathcal{N}

has a
tree structure
, written
\mathsf{Tree}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}},\mathcal{N})
,
if its APS is connected and acyclic.

An HGV program
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet\;M}
has a single type environment, so is
tree-structured; the same goes for child and link threads.
A key feature of HGV is a subformula principle, which states that all
hyper-environments arising in the derivation of an HGV program are
tree-structured.
It follows that a configuration resulting from the reduction of an HGV program
is also tree structured.
Read bottom-up,
\LabTirName
TC-New and
\LabTirName
TC-Par preserve
tree structure, which
is
illustrated by the following two pictures.

\mathcal{G}
\mathcal{N}
\Gamma
\Delta

\mathcal{N}\uplus\{\{z,z^{\prime}\},\{x,y\}\}
\{z,z^{\prime}\}
\{x,y\}
\mathcal{G}
\mathcal{N}
\Gamma,\Delta
\{z,z^{\prime}\}

\mathcal{N}\uplus\{\{z,z^{\prime}\}\}

\mathcal{G}
\mathcal{H}
\mathcal{N}_{1}
\mathcal{N}_{2}

\mathcal{G}
\mathcal{H}
\mathcal{N}_{1}
\mathcal{N}_{2}

\mathcal{N}_{1}\uplus\mathcal{N}_{2}\uplus\{\{x,x^{\prime}\}\}
\{x,x^{\prime}\}

The following lemma states this intuition formally. By analogy to Kleene
equality, we write
\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bumpeq}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\mathcal{Q}
, to mean that either

\mathcal{P}
or
\mathcal{Q}
is undefined, or
\mathcal{P}\iff\mathcal{Q}
.

Lemma 5

(Tree structure)
.

•

\mathsf{Tree}(({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{1}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{2}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}}),\mathcal{N}\uplus\{\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{2}}\}\})~\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bumpeq}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}~\mathsf{Tree}(({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}})),\mathcal{N})

•

\mathsf{Tree}(({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}_{1}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{1}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}),\mathcal{N}_{1})\wedge\mathsf{Tree}(({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}_{2}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{2}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}}),\mathcal{N}_{2})~\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bumpeq}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}~\mathsf{Tree}(({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}_{1}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{1}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}_{2}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{2}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}}),\mathcal{N}_{1}\uplus\mathcal{N}_{2}\uplus\{\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{2}}\}\})

Proof 3.3
.

By the definition of
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bumpeq}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}
, we need only consider the cases where
both sides of the bi-implication are defined.
Both results follow from the observation that adding an edge between two
trees results in a tree, and removing an edge from a tree partitions the
tree into two subtrees.

Tree canonical form

We now define a canonical form for configurations that captures the
tree structure of an APS.
Tree canonical form enables a succinct statement of
open
progress
(Lemma
8
) and a means for embedding HGV
in GV (Proposition
14
).

{defi}
[Tree canonical form]

A configuration
\mathcal{C}
is in
tree canonical form
if it can be written:

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu x_{1}y_{1})(\mathcal{A}_{1}\parallel\cdots\parallel(\nu x_{n}y_{n})(\mathcal{A}_{n}\parallel\phi{N})\cdots)}

where
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{i}}\in\fv({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{A}_{i}})
for
1\leq i\leq n
.

Every well-typed HGV configuration typeable under a single type
environment can be written in tree canonical form.

Theorem 6

(Well-typed configurations in tree canonical forms)
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}
, then there exists some
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}

such that
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}
is in tree canonical
form.

Proof 3.4
.

By induction on the number of
\nu
-binders in
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}
. In the case that

n=0
, it must be the case that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}
for some thread

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
, since parallel composition is only typeable under a hyper-environment
containing two or more type environments. Therefore,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}
is in tree
canonical form by definition.

In the case that
n\geq 1
, by
3
, we can
rewrite the configuration as:

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu x_{1}y_{1})\cdots(\nu x_{n}y_{n})(\circ M_{1}\parallel\cdots\parallel\circ M_{n}\parallel\phi N)}

Fix
\mathcal{N}=\{\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{i}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y_{i}}\}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
.
By definition,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}
has a tree structure with respect to an empty co-name set.
By repeated applications of
TC-New
, there exists some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}
such
that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ M_{1}\parallel\cdots\parallel\circ M_{n}\parallel\phi N}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
;
by Lemma
5
(clause 1, right-to-left),

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}
has a tree
structure.

Construct the APS for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}
using names
\mathcal{N}
; by
Lemma
4
, there exist
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}}\in\mathsf{envs}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}})
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}}
are leaves of the tree and
therefore by the definition of the APS contain precisely one
\nu
-bound name.
By
TC-Par
, there must exist two threads
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}_{2}}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}_{1}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{1}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}_{2}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{2}}
. By runtime type combination,
at least one of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{2}}
must be
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\circ}
; without loss of
generality assume this is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{1}}
. Suppose (again without loss of generality)
that the
\nu
-bound name contained in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{1}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M_{1}}
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu x_{2}y_{2})\cdots(\nu x_{n}y_{n})(\circ M_{2}\parallel\cdots\parallel\circ M_{n}\parallel\phi N)}
.
By Theorem
3
and the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{1}}
is the only

\nu
-bound variable in
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M_{1}}
, we have that

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu x_{1}y_{1})(\circ M_{1}\parallel\mathcal{D})}
.
By the induction hypothesis, there exists some
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}
is in canonical form. By construction we have
that
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu x_{1}y_{1})(\circ M_{1}\parallel\mathcal{D}^{\prime})}
, which
is in tree canonical form as required.

As hyper-environments capture parallelism, a configuration

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}
typeable under hyper-environment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}}\parallel\cdots\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{n}}
is equivalent to
n
independent parallel
processes.

Proposition 7

(Independence)
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}}\parallel\cdots\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{n}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}
, then there exist
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{n}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}_{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}_{n}}

such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{1}}\sqcap\cdots\sqcap{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{n}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}_{1}\parallel\cdots\parallel\mathcal{D}_{n}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{i}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}_{i}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{i}}
for
each
i
.

Proof 3.5
.

By induction on the derivation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}}\parallel\cdots\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{n}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}
.
The cases for
TC-Main
,
TC-Child
, and
TC-Link

follow immediately. The cases for
TC-New
and
TC-Par

follow from the IH and structural congruence rules.

It follows from Theorem
6

and Proposition
7
that any well-typed HGV configuration can be
written as a forest of independent configurations in tree canonical form.

Progress and Deadlock Freedom

With tree canonical forms defined, we can now state a progress result.
A thread is
blocked
on an endpoint
x
if it is ready to
perform a communication action on
x
.

{defi}
[Blocked thread]
We say that thread
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{T}}
is
blocked on variable

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z}
,
written
\mathsf{blocked}({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{T}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z})
, if either:
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{T}}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\;z}
;

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{T}}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x{\overset{z}{\leftrightarrow}}y}
, for some
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
;
or
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{T}}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}F[N]}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}F}
,
where
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{send}\medspace(V,z)}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{recv}\medspace z}
, or
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{wait}\medspace z}
.

We let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Psi}
range over type environments containing only
session-typed variables,
i
.
e
.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Psi}\Coloneqq\cdot\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Psi},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}
, which lets us reason about configurations
that are closed except for runtime names. Using
Lemma
8
we obtain
open progress
for
configurations with free runtime names.

Lemma 8

(Open Progress)
.

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Psi}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
where

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}=\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu x_{1}y_{1})(\mathcal{A}_{1}\parallel\cdots\parallel(\nu x_{n}y_{n})(\mathcal{A}_{n}\parallel\phi N)\cdots)

is in tree canonical form.
Either
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}
, or:

(1)

For each
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{A}_{i}}

(1\leq i\leq n)
,

\mathsf{blocked}({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{A}_{i}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z})
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z}\in\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{i}}\}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y_{j}}\mid 1\leq j<i\}\cup\fv({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Psi})

(2)

Either
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}
is a value or

\mathsf{blocked}({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi N},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z})
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z}\in\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y_{i}}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}\cup\fv({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Psi})

Proof 3.6
.

Open progress follows as a direct corollary of a slightly more verbose property
which holds on HGV processes, proved by induction on the derivation of an inductive definition of tree canonical forms.
See
Appendix

A
for details.

Closed configurations enjoy a stronger result: if a closed configuration cannot reduce, then each auxiliary thread must either be a value, or be blocked on its neighbouring endpoint.

Lemma 9

(Closed Progress)
.

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Psi}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}}

where

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu x_{1}y_{1})(\mathcal{A}_{1}\parallel\cdots\parallel(\nu x_{n}y_{n})(\mathcal{A}_{n}\parallel\phi N)\cdots)}

is in tree canonical form.
Either
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}
, or:

(1)

For each
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{A}_{j}}
for
1\leq j\leq n
,
\mathsf{blocked}({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{A}_{j}}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{j}}})

(2)

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}
is a value

Proof 3.7
.

Since the environment is closed, by Lemma
8
, for
each
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{A}_{j}}
it must be that

\mathsf{blocked}({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{A}_{j}}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z}})
for some

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z}\in\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y_{i}}\mid i\in 1..{j-1}\}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{j}}\}
.

Note that if two names
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
are co-names, and one thread is blocked on

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
, and another is blocked on
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
, then due to typing the names must be dual
and reduction can occur.

Consider
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{A}_{1}}
. Since the environment is closed,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{A}_{1}}
must
be blocked on
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{1}}
. Next, consider
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{A}_{2}}
; the thread cannot be
blocked on
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y_{1}}
as reduction would occur.
By the definition of tree canonical forms,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{A}_{2}}
must contain

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{2}}
and by the typing rules cannot contain
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y_{2}}
, so the thread
must be blocked on
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{2}}
. The argument extends to the remainder of the
configuration.

Finally, for
ground configurations
, where the main thread does
not return a runtime name or capture a runtime name in a closure, we
obtain a yet tighter result,
global progress
, which implies
deadlock freedom
[
CDM14
]
.

{defi}
[Ground configuration]
A configuration
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}
is a
ground configuration
if

\cdot\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}
is in canonical form,
and
T
does not contain session types or function types.

Our main progress result states that a ground configuration can
reduce, or is a value.

Theorem 10

(Global progress)
.

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}
is a ground configuration. Either there exists some

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}
, or
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet V}
for some value
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}
.

Proof 3.8
.

By Lemma
9
, either
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}
can reduce, or

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}
can be written:

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu x_{1}y_{1})(\circ\mathcal{A}_{1}\parallel\cdots\parallel(\nu x_{n}y_{n})(\circ\mathcal{A}_{n}\parallel\bullet V)\cdots)}

where

\mathsf{blocked}({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{A}_{i}}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{i}}})
for each
\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{i}}\mid i\in 1..n\}
.

Since
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}
is ground,
\fv({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V})=\emptyset
. By definition, tree
canonical form ensures that no cycles are present amongst threads, so no
auxiliary thread can be blocked.
It follows that if
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\not\longrightarrow
, then there cannot be any auxiliary
threads and thus
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet V}
for some value
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}
.

Determinism and Strong Normalisation

HGV enjoys a strong form of determinism known as the diamond property,
and due to linearity it enjoys strong normalisation. Unlike with
preservation and progress, the addition of hypersequents does not
substantially change the arguments from
[
LM15
]
.

Theorem 11

(Diamond property)
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}
.

Proof 3.9
.

Similar to that of GV
[
LM15
,
Fow19
]
:

\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{M}}
is deterministic, and due to linearity, any overlapping
reductions are separate and may be performed in either order.

Theorem 12

(Termination)
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
, there are no infinite
sequences
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow\longrightarrow\cdots
.

Proof 3.10
.

As with GV
[
LM15
,
Fow19
]
, due to
linearity, HGV has an elementary strong normalisation proof. Let the
size of a configuration be the sum of the sizes of all abstract
syntax trees of all terms contained in threads. The size of a
configuration is invariant under
\equiv
and strictly decreases
under
\longrightarrow
, so no infinite reduction sequences can exist.

4.
Relation between HGV and GV

In this section, we show that well-typed GV configurations are
well-typed HGV configurations, and well-typed HGV configurations with
tree structure are well-typed GV configurations.

GV

Typing rules for configurations

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\langle{x,y}\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S^{\sharp}}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TG-New}~\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\langle{x,y}\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S^{\sharp}}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}\sqcap{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R^{\prime}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TG-Connect${}_{1}$}~\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\langle{x,y}\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S^{\sharp}}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}\sqcap{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R^{\prime}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TG-Connect${}_{2}$}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{!}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\circ}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TG-Child}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bullet\;T}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TG-Main}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{?}}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x{\overset{z}{\leftrightarrow}}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\circ}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TG-Link}

Figure 5.
GV, typing rules for configurations.

HGV and GV share a common term language and reduction semantics, so
only differ in their runtime typing rules.
Figure

5
gives
the runtime typing rules for GV. We adapt the rules to use a double-binder
formulation to concentrate on the essence of the relationship with HGV, but it
is trivial to translate GV with single binders into GV with double binders.

GV uses a pseudo-type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S^{\sharp}}
to type channels.
Unlike endpoints, channels cannot appear in terms.
Read bottom-up, rule
\LabTirName
TG-New types a name restriction

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)\mathcal{C}}
, adding

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\langle{x,y}\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S^{\sharp}}
to the type environment, which along
with
\LabTirName
TG-Connect
1
and
\LabTirName
TG-Connect
2

ensures that a session channel of type
S
will be split into
endpoints
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
over a parallel composition. In turn, this
enforces a tree process structure. The remaining typing rules are as
in HGV.

A simple embedding of GV into HGV

The simplest embedding of GV in HGV relies on the observation
from
Section

2
that each parallel composition splits
a single channel. Let
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}\parallel_{\langle{x},{y}\rangle}\mathcal{D}}}

denote two configurations
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}
connected
by a channel with endpoints
x,y
. We can write an arbitrary closed GV
configuration in the form:

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}_{1}\parallel_{\langle{x_{1}},{y_{1}}\rangle}\cdots\parallel_{\langle{x_{n-2}},{y_{n-2}}\rangle}\mathcal{C}_{n-1}\parallel_{\langle{x_{n-1}},{y_{n-1}}\rangle}\mathcal{C}_{n}}

where each
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}
does not contain a further parallel
composition, and any main thread is in
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}_{n}}
. We can then
subsequently embed the configuration in HGV as:

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu x_{1}y_{1})(\mathcal{C}_{1}\parallel\cdots\parallel(\nu x_{n-2}y_{n-2})(\mathcal{C}_{n-2}\parallel(\nu x_{n-1}y_{n-1})(\mathcal{C}_{n-1}\parallel\mathcal{C}_{n}))\cdots)}

which is well-typed by construction. As a corollary, every well-typed,
closed GV configuration is equivalent to a well-typed, closed HGV
configuration.

A structure-preserving embedding of GV into HGV

Though the simple embedding of GV into HGV is sound, it does not
respect the
intention
of GV. In fact, we can provide
a stronger result: every well-typed open GV configuration is exactly a
well-typed HGV configuration.

{defi}

[Flattening]
Flattening, written
\mathop{\downarrow}{}
, converts GV type environments and HGV hyper-environments into HGV environments.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\mathop{\downarrow}{\cdot}}&=&{\cdot}\\
{\mathop{\downarrow}{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\langle{x,x^{\prime}}\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S^{\sharp}})}}&=&{\mathop{\downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}}}\\
{\mathop{\downarrow}{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T})}}&=&{\mathop{\downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}\end{array}\qquad\qquad\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\mathop{\downarrow}{\varnothing}}&=&{\varnothing}\\
{\mathop{\downarrow}{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}})}}&=&{\mathop{\downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}}\\
\\
\end{array}\hfill

{defi}

[Splitting]
Splitting converts GV type environments into hyper-environments.
Given channels
{\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\langle{x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i}}\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S_{i}^{\sharp}}\}_{i\in 1..n}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}
,
a hyper-environment

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}}
is a
splitting
of

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}
if
{\mathop{\downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}}}={\mathop{\downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}}
and
\exists{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{n+1}}}
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}}\parallel\cdots\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{n+1}}}
, and
\mathsf{Tree}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}},\{\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}_{1}}\},\ldots,\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x_{n}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}_{n}}\}\})
.

A well-typed GV configuration is typeable in HGV under a splitting of its type environment.

Theorem 13

(Typeability of GV configurations in HGV)
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}}
, then there exists some

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}}
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}}
is a
splitting of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}}
.

Proof 4.1
.

By induction on the derivation of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}
(see
Appendix

B
).

{exa}

Consider a configuration where a child thread pings the main thread:

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)(\circ\;(\mathbf{send}\;(\mathit{ping},x))\parallel\bullet\;(\mathbf{let}\;((),y)=\mathbf{recv}\;y\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{wait}\;y))}}

We can write a GV typing derivation as follows:

\frac{\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!{\mathbf{1}}.{\mathbf{end}_{!}}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathit{ping}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\;(\mathbf{send}\;(\mathit{ping},x))}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\circ}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?{\mathbf{1}}.{\mathbf{end}_{?}}}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet\;(\mathbf{let}\;((),y)\mathbin{=}\mathbf{recv}\;y\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{wait}\;y)}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bullet\;\mathbf{1}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\langle{x,y}\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!{\mathbf{1}}.{\mathbf{end}_{!}}^{\sharp}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathit{ping}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)(\circ(\mathbf{send}\;(\mathit{ping},x))\parallel\bullet(\mathbf{let}\;((),y)\mathbin{=}\mathbf{recv}\;y\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{wait}\;y))}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathit{ping}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)(\circ(\mathbf{send}\;(\mathit{ping},x))\parallel\bullet(\mathbf{let}\;((),y)\mathbin{=}\mathbf{recv}\;y\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{wait}\;y))}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}}

The corresponding HGV derivation is:

\frac{\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!{\mathbf{1}}.{\mathbf{end}_{!}}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathit{ping}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\;(\mathbf{send}\;(\mathit{ping},x))}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\circ}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?{\mathbf{1}}.{\mathbf{end}_{?}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet\;(\mathbf{let}\;((),y)=\mathbf{recv}\;y\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{wait}\;y)}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bullet\;\mathbf{1}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!{\mathbf{1}}.{\mathbf{end}_{!}}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathit{ping}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?{\mathbf{1}}.{\mathbf{end}_{?}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)(\circ(\mathbf{send}\;(\mathit{ping},x))\parallel\bullet(\mathbf{let}\;((),y)=\mathbf{recv}\;y\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{wait}\;y))}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bullet\;\mathbf{1}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathit{ping}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)(\circ(\mathbf{send}\;(\mathit{ping},x))\parallel\bullet(\mathbf{let}\;((),y)=\mathbf{recv}\;y\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{wait}\;y))}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bullet\;\mathbf{1}}}

Note that

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}!{\mathbf{1}}.{\mathbf{end}_{!}}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathit{ping}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}?{\mathbf{1}}.{\mathbf{end}_{?}}}}
is a splitting of

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\langle{x,y}\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}(!{\mathbf{1}}.{\mathbf{end}_{!}})^{\sharp}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathit{ping}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}}
.

Translating HGV to GV

As we saw in §
2
, unlike in HGV, equivalence in GV is not
type-preserving. It follows that HGV types strictly more processes
than GV. Let us revisit Lindley and Morris’ example
from §
