1
(adapted to use double-binders), where

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{3}}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(\nu yy^{\prime})(\mathcal{C}\parallel(\mathcal{D}\parallel\mathcal{E}))}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{1}\sqcap R_{2}\sqcap R_{3}}}

with

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{1}}}
,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S^{\prime}}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{2}}}
,
and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S^{\prime}}}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{E}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{3}}}
.

The structurally-equivalent term

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(\nu yy^{\prime})((\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D})\parallel\mathcal{E})}

is not typeable in GV, since we cannot split both channels over a
single parallel composition:

\frac{\frac{\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}\not\vdash_{\textsf{GV}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{1}\sqcap R_{2}}\hskip 7.3612pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\langle{y,y^{\prime}}\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S^{\prime\sharp}}\not\vdash_{\textsf{GV}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{E}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{3}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\langle{x,x^{\prime}}\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S^{\sharp}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\langle{y,y^{\prime}}\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S^{\prime\sharp}}\not\vdash_{\textsf{GV}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D})\parallel\mathcal{E}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{1}\sqcap R_{2}\sqcap R_{3}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\langle{x,x^{\prime}}\rangle}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S^{\sharp}}\not\vdash_{\textsf{GV}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu yy^{\prime})((\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D})\parallel\mathcal{E})}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{1}\sqcap R_{2}\sqcap R_{3}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{3}}\not\vdash_{\textsf{GV}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(\nu yy^{\prime})((\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D})\parallel\mathcal{E})}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{1}\sqcap R_{2}\sqcap R_{3}}}

However, we
can
type this process in HGV:

\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{1}}\hskip 7.3612pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S^{\prime}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{2}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S^{\prime}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{1}\sqcap R_{2}}}\hskip 7.3612pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S^{\prime}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{E}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{3}}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S^{\prime}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S^{\prime}}}$}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D})\parallel\mathcal{E}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{1}\sqcap R_{2}\sqcap R_{3}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu yy^{\prime})((\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D})\parallel\mathcal{E})}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{1}\sqcap R_{2}\sqcap R_{3}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{3}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(\nu yy^{\prime})((\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D})\parallel\mathcal{E})}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R_{1}\sqcap R_{2}\sqcap R_{3}}}

Note in particular the shaded hyper-environment, which includes
hyper-environment separators to separate endpoints
x
and
x^{\prime}
, as well as
y

and
y^{\prime}
. It follows that, unlike in GV,
both
channels can be split over
the same parallel composition. Similarly, the hyper-environment separator allows

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}
to be composed
without
sharing any
channels.

Although HGV types more processes, every well-typed HGV configuration typeable
under a singleton hyper-environment
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}
is
equivalent
to
a well-typed GV configuration, which we show using tree canonical forms.

Proposition 14
.

Suppose
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}}
where

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}}
is in tree canonical form. Then,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}}
.

Proof 4.2
.

By induction on the derivation of

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}}
, making use of an inductive
definition of tree canonical forms. See
Appendix

B
for
details.

Remark 15
.

It is not the case that every HGV configuration typeable under an

arbitrary
hyper-environment
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}}
is
equivalent to a well-typed GV configuration. This is because open
HGV configurations can form
forest
process structures,
whereas (even open) GV configurations must form a
tree

process structure.

Since we can write all well-typed HGV configurations in canonical
form, and HGV tree canonical forms are typeable in GV, it
follows that every well-typed HGV configuration typeable under a
single type environment is equivalent to a well-typed GV
configuration.

Corollary 16
.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}}
, then there exists some

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash_{\text{{GV}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{D}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}}
.

5.
Relation between HGV and HCP

In this section, we explore two translations, from HGV to HCP and from
HCP to HGV, together with their operational correspondence results.

Hypersequent CP

HCP
[
MP18
,
KMP19b
]
is a session-typed process
calculus with a correspondence to CLL, which exploits hypersequents to
fix extensibility and modularity issues with CP.

Typing rules for processes

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}}

\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}{\leftrightarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}}{y}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A^{\bot}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TP-Link}~~\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A^{\bot}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){P}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TP-New}~~\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel Q}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TP-Par}~~\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{0}}\vdash{\varnothing}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TP-Halt}~~\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{\varnothing}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[].P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TP-Close}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x().P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bot}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TP-Wait}~~\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[y].P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\otimes}{B}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TP-Send}~~\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x(y).P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\invamp}{B}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TP-Recv}~~\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleright\{\}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\top}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TP-Offer-Absurd}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inl}.{P}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\oplus}{B}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TP-Select-Inl}~~\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inr}.{P}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\oplus}{B}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TP-Select-Inr}~~\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleright\{\text{inl}:P;\text{inr}:Q\}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\with}{B}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=TP-Offer}

Duality

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}^{\bot}

\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}({A}\mathbin{\otimes}{B})^{\bot}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A^{\bot}}\mathbin{\invamp}{B^{\bot}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}({A}\mathbin{\invamp}{B})^{\bot}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A^{\bot}}\mathbin{\otimes}{B^{\bot}}}\end{array}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}(\mathbf{1})^{\bot}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bot}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}(\bot)^{\bot}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}\end{array}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}({A}\mathbin{\oplus}{B})^{\bot}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A^{\bot}}\mathbin{\with}{B^{\bot}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}({A}\mathbin{\with}{B})^{\bot}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A^{\bot}}\mathbin{\oplus}{B^{\bot}}}\end{array}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}(\mathbf{0})^{\bot}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\top}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}(\top)^{\bot}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{0}}\end{array}

Figure 6.
HCP, duality and typing rules for processes.

Types (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B}
) consist of the connectives of
linear logic: the multiplicative operators (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\otimes}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\invamp}
) and units (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bot}
) and the
additive operators (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\oplus}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\with}
) and units
(
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{0}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\top}
).

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}B}\Coloneqq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bot}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{0}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\top}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\otimes}{B}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\invamp}{B}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\oplus}{B}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{A}\mathbin{\with}{B}}

Type environments (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}
) associate names with
types. Hyper-environments (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}
) are
collections of type environments. The empty type environment and
hyper-environment are written
\cdot
and
\varnothing
,
respectively. Names in type and hyper-environments must be unique
and environments may be combined, written
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Delta}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}
, only if they are
disjoint.

Processes (
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}
) are a variant of the
\pi
-calculus
with forwarding
[
San96
,
Bor98
]
, bound
output
[
San96
]
, and double
binders
[
Vas12
]
.
The syntax of processes is given by the typing rules
(
Figure

6
), which are standard for
HCP
[
MP18
,
KMP19b
]
:

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}{\leftrightarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}}{y}}
forwards messages between
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
;

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){P}}
creates a channel with endpoints
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
, and continues as
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}
;

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel Q}
composes
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}
in parallel;

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{0}}
is the terminated process;

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[y].P}
creates a new channel, outputs one endpoint over
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
, binds the other to
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
, and continues as
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}
;

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x(y).P}
receives a channel endpoint, binds it to
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
, and continues as
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}
;

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[].P}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x().P}
close
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
and continue as
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}
;

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inl}.{P}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inr}.{P}}
make a binary choice;

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleright\{\text{inl}:P;\text{inr}:Q\}}
offers a binary choice;
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleright\{\}}
offers a nullary choice.
As HCP is synchronous, the only difference between
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[y].P}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x(y).P}
is their typing (and similarly for
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[].P}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x().P}
).
We write
unbound
send as
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x\langle{y}\rangle.P}
(short for

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[z].({y}{\leftrightarrow}{z}\parallel P)}
), and synchronisation as

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bar{x}.P}
(short for

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[z].(z[].\mathbf{0}\parallel P)}
) and

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x.P}
(short for
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x(z).z().P}
).
Duality is standard and is involutive,
i
.
e
.,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}(A^{\bot})^{\bot}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}
.

We define a standard structural congruence (
\equiv
) similar to that
of HGV,
i
.
e
., parallel composition is commutative and associative, we
can commute name restrictions, swap the order of endpoints, swap
links, and have scope extrusion (similar to
Figure

4
).
Note that since we base our formal developments on an LTS semantics, structural
congruence is not required for reduction.

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}{\leftrightarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A}}{y}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{y}{\leftrightarrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}A^{\bot}}}{x}}\par{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel\mathbf{0}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}\par{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel Q}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q\parallel P}\par{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel(Q\parallel R)}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(P\parallel Q)\parallel R}\par{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(\nu yy^{\prime})P}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu yy^{\prime})(\nu xx^{\prime})P}\par{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)P}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu yx)P}\par{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)(P\parallel Q)}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel(\nu xy){Q}}\quad\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}\not\in\fv({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P})

Action rules

\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\pi.P}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\pi}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=Act-Pref}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}{\leftrightarrow}{y}}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}{\leftrightarrow}{y}}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{0}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=Act-Link\textsubscript{1}}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}{\leftrightarrow}{y}}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{y}{\leftrightarrow}{x}}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{0}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=Act-Link\textsubscript{2}}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleright\{\text{inl}:P;\text{inr}:Q\}}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleright\text{inl}}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=Act-Off-Inl}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleright\{\text{inl}:P;\text{inr}:Q\}}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleright\text{inr}}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=Act-Off-Inr}

Communication Rules

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}{\leftrightarrow}{z}}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){P}}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\alpha}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}\{z/y\}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=Alp-Link}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[x^{\prime}]\parallel y(y^{\prime})}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){P}}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\beta}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){(\nu{x^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}}){P^{\prime}}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=Bet-Send}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[]\parallel y()}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){P}}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\beta}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=Bet-Close}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inl}\parallel{y}\triangleright\text{inl}}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){P}}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\beta}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){P^{\prime}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=Bet-Inl}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inr}\parallel{y}\triangleright\text{inr}}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){P}}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\beta}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){P^{\prime}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=Bet-Inr}

Structural Rules

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}\not\in\cn({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell})}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){P}}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){P^{\prime}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=Str-Res}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}\bn({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell})\cap\fn({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q})=\varnothing}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel Q}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}\parallel Q}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=Str-Par\textsubscript{1}}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q^{\prime}}\bn({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell})\cap\fn({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P})=\varnothing}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel Q}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel Q^{\prime}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=Str-Par\textsubscript{2}}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell^{\prime}}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q^{\prime}}\bn(\ell)\cap\bn(\ell^{\prime})=\varnothing}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P\parallel Q}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}l\parallel l^{\prime}}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}\parallel Q^{\prime}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{lab=Str-Syn}

Figure 7.
HCP, label transition semantics.

We define the labelled transition system for HCP as a small refinement of the
LTS for the additive-multiplicative fragment of the
\pi\text{LL}
calculus
introduced by Montesi and Peressotti
[
MP21
]
, in turn inspired by
their previous system
CT

[
MP18
]
. The LTS is identical, save
for the fact that we distinguish two types of internal actions.
Action labels
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}l}
represent the actions that a
process can fire.
Prefixes
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\pi}
are a convenient subset of action labels which can be
written as prefixes to processes,
i
.
e
.,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\pi.P}
.
Transition labels
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}
include action labels and the parallel
composition of two action labels, along with
internal
actions

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\alpha}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\beta}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\tau}
.
The LTS gives rise to two types of internal action:
\alpha
represents only the
evaluation of links as
renaming
, and
\beta
represents only

communication
.
Labels
\tau
arise only due to saturated transition
(Definition
5
) and are not produced by the rules in
the LTS.

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\text{Prefixes}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\pi}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[y]}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[]}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x(y)}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x()}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inl}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleleft\text{inr}}\\
\text{Action Labels}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}l}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\pi}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}{\leftrightarrow}{y}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleright\text{inl}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}\triangleright\text{inr}}\\
\text{Transition Labels}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}l}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}l\parallel l^{\prime}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\alpha}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\beta}\end{array}

We let
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell_{x}}
range over labels on
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
:

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{x}{\leftrightarrow}{y}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[y]}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x[]}
,

etc
. Labelled transition
\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}}{\longrightarrow}
is defined
in
Figure

7
. We write
\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}}{\longrightarrow}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell^{\prime}}}{\longrightarrow}
for the
composition of
\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}}{\longrightarrow}
and
\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell^{\prime}}}{\longrightarrow}
,
\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell^{+}}}{\longrightarrow}
for the
transitive closure of
\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}}{\longrightarrow}
, and
\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell^{*}}}{\longrightarrow}
for the
reflexive-transitive closure of
\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}}{\longrightarrow}
. We write

\bn({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell})
and
\fn({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell})
for the bound and free names
contained in
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}
, respectively. We write
\cn({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell})
for
all names in
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}
,
i
.
e
.,
\cn({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell})=\fn({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell})\cup\bn({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell})
.

Metatheory

Transitions preserve typeability. Since internal actions occur only under binders,
they are typable under the same hyper-environment.

Theorem 17

(Type Preservation)
.

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}
.

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}
is internal, then
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}}
.

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}
is not internal, then there exists some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}
such
that
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}}
.

Proof 5.1
.

Following the approach of
[
KMP19a
,
MP18
,
MP21
]
, type preservation is
established by defining proof transformations on typing derivations of each
reducing process. The only difference with respect to
[
MP18
,
MP21
]
arises due to our
separate treatment of
\alpha
and
\beta
actions, which does not
materially impact the proof.

Similarly, our LTS for HCP satisfies progress.
Following
[
KMP19a
,
MP21
]
, the key intermediate
step is to note that for every type environment in a
hyper-environment, there is some free name which can be acted
upon. Again, the stratification of internal actions does not
materially impact the proof.

Theorem 18

(Progress)
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\not\equiv{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{0}}
, then there
exist some
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}
.

Behavioural Theory

The behavioural theory for HCP follows Kokke
et al
.
[
KMP19a
]
, except
that we distinguish two subrelations of weak bisimilarity, following the subtypes of internal actions.

{defi}
[Strong bisimulation and strong bisimilarity]
A symmetric relation
\mathcal{R}
on processes is a
strong bisimulation

if
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\mathbin{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}
implies that if

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q^{\prime}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q^{\prime}}

such that
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}\mathbin{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q^{\prime}}
.
Strong bisimilarity
is
the largest relation
\sim
that is a strong bisimulation.

{defi}
[Saturated transition]
The
\mathcal{L}
-saturated transition relation, for
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\alpha},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\beta}\}
, is the smallest relation
\overset{}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{L}}
closed under the following rules, with saturated transition labels
\ell
ranging over transition labels and the distinguished label
\tau
:

\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{\tau}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{L}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{\tau}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{L}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q^{\prime}}\overset{\tau}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{L}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}\quad\ell\in\mathcal{L}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{\tau}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{L}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{\tau}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{L}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q^{\prime}}\overset{\tau}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{L}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}\quad\ell\notin\mathcal{L}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{\ell}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{L}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}}

We write
\overset{}{\Longrightarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}}
as shorthand for
\overset{}{\Longrightarrow}_{\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\ell}\}}
, and
we write
\overset{}{\Longrightarrow}
as shorthand for
\overset{}{\Longrightarrow}_{\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\alpha},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\beta}\}}
.

{defi}
[Weak bisimulation and weak bisimilarity]

A symmetric relation
\mathcal{R}
on processes is an
\mathcal{L}
-bisimulation, for

\mathcal{L}\subseteq\{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\alpha},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\beta}\}
, if
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\mathbin{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}
implies that
if
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{\ell^{\prime}}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{L}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}
, then

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}\overset{\ell^{\prime}}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{L}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q^{\prime}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q^{\prime}}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}\mathbin{\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q^{\prime}}
.
The
\mathcal{L}
-bisimilarity relation is the
largest relation
\approx_{\mathcal{L}}
that is an
\mathcal{L}
-bisimulation.
We write
\approx
as shorthand for
\approx_{\{\alpha,\beta\}}
.

Lemma 19
.

Structural congruence, strong bisimilarity and the various forms of weak
bisimilarity are related as follows:

{\equiv}\subset{\sim}\par{\sim}\subset{\approx}\par{\sim}\subset{\approx_{\alpha}}\par{\sim}\subset{\approx_{\beta}}

Differences with previous version

The LTS in
Figure

7
is similar to that in the previous version of this
work
[
FKD+21
]
, with the exception that we have removed the rules

\LabTirName
Tau-Alp and
\LabTirName
Tau-Bet:

\cancel{\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\alpha}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\tau}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}}\quad\textsc{lab=Tau-Alp}}\par\cancel{\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\beta}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\tau}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}}\quad\textsc{lab=Tau-Bet}}

To see why these rules are problematic, consider processes

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{y}){({z}{\leftrightarrow}{x}\parallel y[].\mathbf{0})}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z[].\mathbf{0}}
. Following
Section

5
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}
are
\alpha
-bisimilar, as
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}
only has the
\alpha
-transition

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\alpha}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}
has no transitions. In the previous version,

\LabTirName
Tau-Alp gave
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}
the derived
\tau
-transition

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\tau}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}
, which meant that
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\not\approx_{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}
, as

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 6.98279pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\overset{\tau}{\Longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 6.98279pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\overset{\tau}{\Longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.32901pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\overset{\tau}{\Longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.57901pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\overset{\tau}{\Longrightarrow}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}Q}
. Therefore
\LabTirName
Tau-Alp collapses
\approx_{\alpha}

to
\sim
and
\LabTirName
Tau-Bet collapses
\approx_{\beta}
to
\sim
.

The solution we adopted was to remove
\LabTirName
Tau-Alp and
\LabTirName
Tau-Bet
from the label transition relation
\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}}}{\longrightarrow}
, and instead lift
\alpha
- and

\beta
-transitions to
\tau
-transitions in the definition of saturated
transition
1
1

1

We thank Marco Peressotti for notifying us of the error and
suggesting the fix.
.

Translating HGV to HCP

We factor the translation from HGV to HCP into two translations: (1) a
translation into
\text{HGV}*
, a fine-grain call-by-value
[
LPT03
]

variant of HGV, which makes control flow explicit; and (2) a
translation from
\text{HGV}*
to HCP. In so doing, we can concentrate on the essence
of the translations as opposed to concerning ourselves with administrative
reductions.

\text{HGV}*

We define
\text{HGV}*
as a refinement of HGV in which any non-trivial term
must be named by a let-binding before being used.
While
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}}}
is syntactic sugar in HGV, it is part of the core language
in
\text{HGV}*
. Correspondingly, the reduction rule for

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}}}
follows from
the encoding in HGV,
i
.
e
.,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;x=V\;\mathbf{in}\;M}\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{M}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M\{V/x\}}}
.

\small\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\text{Terms}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;x=M\;\mathbf{in}\;N}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V\;W}\\
&&\;\mid&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;()=V\;\mathbf{in}\;M}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;(x,y)=V\;\mathbf{in}\;M}\\
&&\;\mid&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{absurd}\;V}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{case}\;V\left\{\mathbf{inl}\;x\mapsto{M};\;\mathbf{inr}\;y\mapsto{N}\right\}}\\
\text{Values}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}W}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}K}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda x.M}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}()}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(V,W)}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inl}\;V}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{inr}\;V}\\
\text{Evaluation contexts}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\square}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;x=E\;\mathbf{in}\;M}\\
\text{Thread contexts}&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{F}}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\phi\;E}\end{array}

Remark 20
.

Fine-grain call-by-value
\lambda
-calculi typically include an explicit

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{return}\medspace V}}
construct to embed values into the term language.
As there is no difference between the shapes of the value and term typing
judgements, we allow ourselves to embed values directly for simplicity.

We can
naïvely
translate HGV to
\text{HGV}*
(
\llparenthesis{\cdot}\rrparenthesis
) by
let-binding each subterm in a value position,
e
.
g
.,

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis{\mathbf{inl}\;M}\rrparenthesis}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;z=\llparenthesis{M}\rrparenthesis\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{inl}\;z}}
.

{defi}

[Naïve translation of HGV to
\text{HGV}*
]

\hskip-5.0pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis{x}\rrparenthesis}}&\!=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis{\lambda x.M}\rrparenthesis}}&\!=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\lambda x.\llparenthesis{M}\rrparenthesis}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis{L\;M}\rrparenthesis}}&\!=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;x=\llparenthesis{L}\rrparenthesis\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{let}\;y=\llparenthesis{M}\rrparenthesis\;\mathbf{in}\;x\;y}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis{()}\rrparenthesis}}&\!=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}()}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis{\mathbf{let}\;()=L\;\mathbf{in}\;M}\rrparenthesis}}&\!=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;z=\llparenthesis{L}\rrparenthesis\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{let}\;()=z\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis{M}\rrparenthesis}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis{(M,N)}\rrparenthesis}}&\!=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;x=\llparenthesis{M}\rrparenthesis\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{let}\;y=\llparenthesis{N}\rrparenthesis\;\mathbf{in}\;(x,y)}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis{\mathbf{let}\;(x,y)=L\;\mathbf{in}\;M}\rrparenthesis}}&\!=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;z=\llparenthesis{L}\rrparenthesis\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{let}\;(x,y)=z\;\mathbf{in}\;\llparenthesis{M}\rrparenthesis}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis{\mathbf{inl}\;M}\rrparenthesis}}&\!=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;z=\llparenthesis{M}\rrparenthesis\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{inl}\;z}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis{\mathbf{inr}\;M}\rrparenthesis}}&\!=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;z=\llparenthesis{M}\rrparenthesis\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{inr}\;z}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis{\mathbf{case}\;L\left\{\mathbf{inl}\;x\mapsto{M};\;\mathbf{inr}\;y\mapsto{N}\right\}}\rrparenthesis}}&\!=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;z=\llparenthesis{L}\rrparenthesis\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{case}\;z\left\{\mathbf{inl}\;x\mapsto{\llparenthesis{M}\rrparenthesis};\;\mathbf{inr}\;y\mapsto{\llparenthesis{N}\rrparenthesis}\right\}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llparenthesis{\mathbf{absurd}\;L}\rrparenthesis}}&\!=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;z=\llparenthesis{L}\rrparenthesis\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{absurd}\;z}}\\
\end{array}

Standard techniques can be used to avoid administrative
redexes
[
Plo75
,
DMN07
]
.
We give a full definition of
\text{HGV}*
in Appendix
C
.

\text{HGV}*
to HCP

The translation from
\text{HGV}*
to HCP is given in

Figure

8
. All control flow is encapsulated in values
and let-bindings. We define a pair of translations on types,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llfloor\cdot\rrfloor}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llceil\cdot\rrceil}}
, such
that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llfloor T\rrfloor}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llceil T\rrceil^{\bot}}}
. We extend
these translations pointwise to type environments and
hyper-environments. We define translations on configurations
(
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{c}}}}
), terms (
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}}
) and
values (
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}}}
), where
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}
is a fresh name
denoting a distinguished output channel.

We translate an HGV sequent

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}
as

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathcal{C}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{c}}}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llfloor\mathcal{G}\rrfloor\parallel\llfloor\Gamma\rrfloor},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llfloor T\rrfloor^{\bot}}}}
,
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}
is the type environment corresponding to the main
thread.
The translation of computations includes synchronisation action in
order to faithfully simulate a call-by-value reduction strategy.
The (term) translation of a value
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{V}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}}}

immediately pings the output channel
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}
to announce that it is a
value. The translation of a let-binding

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathbf{let}\;w=M\;\mathbf{in}\;N}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}}}
first evaluates
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}

to a value, which then pings the internal channel
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}/{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}
and
unblocks the continuation
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x.\llbracket{N}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}}}
.
The translations of main and child threads each make use of an
internal result channel.
The translation of a child thread consumes the yielded unit endpoint
once the child thread has terminated.
The translation of the main thread forwards the result value along the
external output channel once the main thread has terminated.

Translation on types

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llfloor T\rrfloor}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llceil T\rrceil}

\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c@{}}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{~}c@{~}l@{}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llfloor!{T}.{S}\rrfloor}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\llfloor T\rrfloor^{\bot}}\mathbin{\otimes}{\llfloor S\rrfloor}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llfloor?{T}.{S}\rrfloor}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\llfloor T\rrfloor^{\bot}}\mathbin{\invamp}{\llfloor S\rrfloor}}}\\
\end{array}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{~}c@{~}l@{}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llfloor\mathbf{end}_{!}\rrfloor}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llfloor\mathbf{end}_{?}\rrfloor}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bot}}\\
\end{array}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{~}c@{~}l@{}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llfloor T\rrfloor}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llceil T\rrceil^{\bot}}},\\
&&\text{if ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}$ is not a session type}\end{array}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c@{}}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{~}c@{~}l@{}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llceil{T}\mathbin{\times}{U}\rrceil}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\llceil T\rrceil}\mathbin{\otimes}{\llceil U\rrceil}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llceil{T}\mathbin{+}{U}\rrceil}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\llceil T\rrceil}\mathbin{\oplus}{\llceil U\rrceil}}}\\
\end{array}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{~}c@{~}l@{}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llceil\mathbf{1}\rrceil}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llceil\mathbf{0}\rrceil}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{0}}}\\
\end{array}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{~}c@{~}l@{}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llceil{T}\mathbin{\multimap}{U}\rrceil}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\llceil T\rrceil^{\bot}}\mathbin{\invamp}{({\mathbf{1}}\mathbin{\otimes}{\llceil U\rrceil})}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llceil S\rrceil}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llfloor S\rrfloor^{\bot}}}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

Translation on configurations, terms, and values

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{c}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}}}

\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\qquad}l@{\qquad}l@{}}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{~}c@{~}l@{}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\circ\;M}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{c}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{y}{y^{\prime}}){(\llbracket{M}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}\parallel y^{\prime}.y^{\prime}[].\mathbf{0})}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\bullet\;M}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{c}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{y}{y^{\prime}}){(\llbracket{M}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}\parallel y^{\prime}.{y^{\prime}}{\leftrightarrow}{r})}}}\\
\end{array}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{~}c@{~}l@{}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{(\nu xx^{\prime})\mathcal{C}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{c}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{x^{\prime}}){\llbracket{\mathcal{C}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{c}}}}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\,\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{c}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathcal{C}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{c}}}\parallel\llbracket{\mathcal{D}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{c}}}}}\\
\end{array}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{~}c@{~}l@{}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{x{\overset{z}{\leftrightarrow}}y}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{c}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bar{z}.z().{x}{\leftrightarrow}{y}}}\\
\\
\end{array}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\qquad}l@{\qquad}l@{}}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{~}c@{~}l@{}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{x}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{{r}{\leftrightarrow}{x}}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\lambda x.M}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{r(x).\llbracket{M}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}}}}\\
\end{array}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{~}c@{~}l@{}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{()}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{r[].\mathbf{0}}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{(V,W)}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r[x].(\llbracket{V}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}}\parallel\llbracket{W}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}})}}\\
\end{array}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{~}c@{~}l@{}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathbf{inl}\;V}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{r}\triangleleft\text{inl}.{\llbracket{V}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}}}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathbf{inr}\;V}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{r}\triangleleft\text{inr}.{\llbracket{V}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}}}}}\end{array}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{~}c@{~}l@{}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{V\;W}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{x^{\prime}}){(\nu{y}{y^{\prime}}){(y\langle{x}\rangle.{{r}{\leftrightarrow}{y}}\parallel\llbracket{V}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}}\parallel\llbracket{W}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}})}}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathbf{let}\;()=V\;\mathbf{in}\;M}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{x^{\prime}}){({x().\llbracket{M}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}}\parallel\llbracket{V}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}})}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathbf{let}\;(x,y)=V\;\mathbf{in}\;M}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{y}{y^{\prime}}){({y(x).\llbracket{M}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}}\parallel\llbracket{V}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y^{\prime}}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}})}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathbf{case}\;V\left\{\mathbf{inl}\;x\mapsto{M};\;\mathbf{inr}\;y\mapsto{N}\right\}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{x^{\prime}}){({{x}\triangleright\{\text{inl}:\llbracket{M}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}};\text{inr}:\llbracket{N\{x/y\}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}\}}\parallel\llbracket{V}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}})}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathbf{absurd}\;V}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{x^{\prime}}){({{x}\triangleright\{\}}\parallel\llbracket{V}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}})}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathbf{let}\;x=M\;\mathbf{in}\;N}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{x}{x^{\prime}}){(x.\llbracket{N}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}\parallel\llbracket{M}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}})}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{V}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bar{r}.\llbracket{V}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}}}}\\
\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{~}c@{~}l@{}}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathbf{link}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r(y).y(x).\bar{r}.r().{x}{\leftrightarrow}{y}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathbf{fork}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{y}{y^{\prime}}){(r(x).y\langle{x}\rangle.\bar{r}.{r}{\leftrightarrow}{y}\parallel y^{\prime}(x).x\langle{y^{\prime}}\rangle.x.x[].\mathbf{0}})}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathbf{send}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r(y).y(x).y\langle{x}\rangle.\bar{r}.{r}{\leftrightarrow}{y}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathbf{recv}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r(x).x(y).\bar{r}.r\langle{y}\rangle.{r}{\leftrightarrow}{x}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathbf{wait}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}}}}&=&{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r(x).x().\bar{r}.r[].\mathbf{0}}}\\
\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 8.
Translation from
\text{HGV}*
to HCP.

There are two changes with respect to the translation of our earlier
paper
[
FKD+21
]
.
First, in the earlier work the translation of the main thread output
directly to the external output channel instead of forwarding via an
intermediary as in the current translation. This change is purely
aesthetic.
Second, in the earlier work the translation of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\mathbf{fork}}}
was
not sufficiently concurrent. Correspondingly there was an error in the
case of the operational correspondence proof which is fixed in the
current paper.

Lemma 21

(Type Preservation)
.

(1)

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}
, then
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{V}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llfloor\Gamma\rrfloor,{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llfloor T\rrfloor^{\bot}}}}}
.

(2)

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}
, then
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{M}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llfloor\Gamma\rrfloor,{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\mathbf{1}}\mathbin{\otimes}{\llfloor T\rrfloor^{\bot}}}}}}
.

(3)

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}\parallel\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}
, where
\Gamma
is the type environment for the main thread in
\mathcal{C}
, then
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathcal{C}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{c}}}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llfloor\mathcal{G}\rrfloor\parallel\llfloor\Gamma\rrfloor,{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\llfloor T\rrfloor^{\bot}}}}}
.

Lemma 22

(Substitution)
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
is a well-typed term with
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}w}\in\fv({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M})
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}V}
is a well-typed value, then
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu{w}{w^{\prime}}){(\llbracket{M}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}\parallel\llbracket{V}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}w^{\prime}}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{v}}})}}\approx_{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{M\{V/w\}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{m}}}}}
.

Theorem 23

(Operational Correspondence)
.

Suppose
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}}
is a well-typed configuration.

(1)

(Preservation of reductions)
If
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}}}
, then there exists a
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}
such that

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathcal{C}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{c}}}}\mathbin{\overset{\beta^{+}}{\Longrightarrow}_{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\approx_{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{c}}}}}
; and

(2)

(Reflection of transitions)

•

if
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathcal{C}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{c}}}}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\alpha}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}
, then

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\approx_{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathcal{C}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{c}}}}}
; and

•

if
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathcal{C}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{c}}}}\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\beta}}{\longrightarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}}
, then
there exists a
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}}
and a
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}}

such that
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P}\mathbin{\overset{\beta^{*}}{\Longrightarrow}_{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}P^{\prime}}\approx_{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\llbracket{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}r}}^{\!\medspace{\mathsf{c}}}}}
.
Furthermore,
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}}
is unique up to structural congruence.

The proof is in Appendix
C
.
One might strive for a tighter operational correspondence here, but
our current translation generates multiple administrative

\beta
-transitions.
The only term reduction that translates to multiple

\beta
-transitions is the one for let-bindings. This is because we
choose to encode synchronisation using two
\beta
-transitions. We
could adjust the accounting here by treating synchronisation as a
single
\beta
-transition or its own special kind of administrative
transition.
Many more administrative reductions arise from the configuration
translation. These are due to a combination of synchronisations and
also the fact that we use constants along with pairs and application
for our communication primitives instead of building-in fully-applied
communication primitives.

Translating HCP to HGV

We cannot translate HCP processes to HGV terms directly: HGV’s term
language only supports
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{fork}}}

(see
Section

7
for further discussion),
so there is no way to translate an individual
name restriction or parallel composition. However, we can still translate HCP
into HGV via the composition of known translations.

HCP into CP:

We must first reunite each
parallel composition with its corresponding name restriction,
i
.
e
.,
translate to CP using the
disentanglement

translation shown by Kokke
et al.

[
KMP19b
, Lemma 4.7]
.
The result is a collection of independent CP processes.

CP into GV:

Next, we can translate each CP process into a GV configuration using (a
variant of) Lindley and Morris’ translation
[
LM15
, Figure 8]
.

GV into HGV:

Finally, we can use our embedding of GV into HGV (
13
)
to obtain a collection of well-typed HGV configurations, which can be
composed using
TC-Par
to result in a single well-typed HGV
configuration.

The translation from HCP into CP and the embedding of GV into HGV preserve and
reflect reduction. However, as previously mentioned, Lindley and Morris’s original translation from CP to
GV preserves but does
not
reflect reduction due to an asynchronous encoding of
choice.
By adapting their translation to use a synchronous encoding of choice
(
Section

3
), we obtain a translation from CP to GV that both
preserves and reflects reduction.
Thus, composing all three translations
together we obtain a translation from HCP to HGV that preserves and reflects
reduction.

6.
Extensions

In this section, we outline three extensions to HGV that exploit
generalising the tree structure of processes to a forest structure.
These extensions are of particular interest since
HGV already supports a core aspect of forest structure, enabling its
full utilisation merely through the addition of a structural rule.
In contrast, to extend GV with forest structure one must distinguish
two distinct introduction rules for parallel
composition
[
LM15
,
Fow19
]
.
Other extensions to GV such as shared
channels
[
LM15
]
, polymorphism
[
LM17
]
,
and recursive session types
[
LM16
]
adapt to HGV
almost unchanged.

From trees to forests

The
\LabTirName
TC-Par rule allows two processes to be composed in parallel
if they are typeable under separate hyper-environments. In a closed program,
hyper-environment separators are introduced by
\LabTirName
TC-Res, meaning that
each process must be connected by a channel.

The following
TC-Mix
rule allows two type environments
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}}
to be
split by a hyper-environment separator
without
a channel connecting them,
and is inspired by Girard’s
Mix
rule
[
Gir87
]
; in the concurrent
setting,
Mix
can be interpreted as concurrency
without
communication

[
LM15
,
ALM16
]
.
\LabTirName
TC-Mix admits a much simpler treatment
of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}
and provides a crucial ingredient for handling exceptional behaviour.

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathcal{C}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}R}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{TC-Mix}

Atkey
et al.

[
ALM16
]
show that conflating the
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}}

and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\bot}}

types in CP (which correspond respectively to the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{!}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{?}}
types
in GV) is logically equivalent to adding the
Mix
rule and a

0-Mix
rule (used to type an empty process).
It follows that in the presence of
TC-Mix
, we use self-dual

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}}
type; in the GV setting, by using a self-dual
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}}
type, we
decouple closing a channel from process termination. We therefore refine the

\LabTirName
TC-Child rule and the type schema for
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{fork}}
to ensure that
each child thread returns the unit value, and replace the
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{wait}}
constant with
a
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{close}}
constant which eliminates an endpoint of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}}
.

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{fork}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{({S}\mathbin{\multimap}{\mathbf{1}})}\mathbin{\multimap}{\overline{S}}}\par{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{close}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\mathbf{end}}\mathbin{\multimap}{\mathbf{1}}}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\circ}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{TC-Child}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xy)(E[\mathbf{close}\;x]\parallel E^{\prime}[\mathbf{close}\;y])}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}E[()]\parallel E^{\prime}[()]}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{E-Close}

Given
\LabTirName
TC-Mix, we might expect a term-level construct
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{spawn}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{({\mathbf{1}}\mathbin{\multimap}{\mathbf{1}})}\mathbin{\multimap}{\mathbf{1}}}
which spawns a parallel thread without a
connecting channel. We can encode such a construct using
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{fork}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{close}}

(assuming fresh
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
):

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{spawn}\;M}\triangleq{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;x=\mathbf{fork}(\lambda y.\mathbf{close}\;y;\;M)\;\mathbf{in}\;}\;{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{close}\;x}}

Assuming the encoded
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{spawn}}
is running in a main thread, after two
reduction steps, we are left with the configuration:

\frac{\frac{\frac{\cdot\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}}{\cdot\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\circ}}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{right=TC-Child}\hskip 8.04996pt\frac{\cdot\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}}{\cdot\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet()}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{right=TC-Main}}{\cdot\parallel\cdot\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ M\parallel\bullet()}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=TC-Par}}{\cdot\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ M\parallel\bullet()}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{1}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right=TC-Mix}

Note the essential use of
\LabTirName
TC-Mix to insert a hyper-environment separator.

The addition of
\LabTirName
TC-Mix does not affect preservation or progress.
The result follows from routine adaptations of the proof
of
3
and
10
.

By relaxing the tree process structure restriction using
\LabTirName
TC-Mix,
we can obtain a more efficient treatment of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}
, and can support the
treatment of exceptions advocated by Fowler
et al.

[
FLMD19
]
.

A simpler link

The
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}\;(x,y)}
construct forwards messages from
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}

and vice-versa.
Consider threads
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}={\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}F[{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}\;(x,y)]}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}
connects to
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
by
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
and to
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}
by

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
.

L
M
N
\{x,x^{\prime}\}
\{y,y^{\prime}\}

\longrightarrow

L
M
N
\{y,y^{\prime}\}

The result of link reduction has forest structure.
Well-typed closed programs in both GV and HGV must
always

maintain tree structure. Different versions of GV do so in various
unsatisfactory ways: one is pre-emptive
blocking
[
LM15
]
, which breaks confluence; another
is two-stage linking (
Figure

4
), which defers
forwarding via a special link thread
[
LM16
]
.

Pre-emptive blocking

Lindley and Morris
[
LM15
]
implement
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}
using the
following rule (modified here to use a double-binder formulation):

{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(F[\mathbf{link}\;(x,y)]\parallel\mathcal{F}^{\prime}[M])}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(F[x]\parallel\mathcal{F}^{\prime}[\mathbf{wait}\;x^{\prime};M\{y/x^{\prime}\}])}}\quad{\text{where }{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}\in\fv({\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M})}

The first thread will eventually reduce to
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ x}
, at which point the second
thread will synchronise to eliminate
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}
and then evaluate
the continuation
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
with endpoint
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
substituted for
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}
.
Unfortunately, this formulation of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}
preemptively inhibits reduction in
the second thread, since the evaluation rule inserts a blocking
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{wait}}
. The
resulting system does not satisfy the diamond property.

Link threads

HGV uses the incarnation of
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}
advocated by Lindley and
Morris
[
LM16
]
, where linking is split into two
stages: the first generates a fresh pair of endpoints
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z^{\prime}}
and a link thread of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x{\overset{z^{\prime}}{\leftrightarrow}}y}
,
and returns
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z}
to the calling thread. Once the calling thread
has evaluated to a value (which must by typing be
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}z}
), then the
link substitution can take place.
This formulation recovers confluence, but we still lose a degree of concurrency:
communication on
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
is blocked until the linking thread has fully evaluated. In
an ideal implementation, the behaviour of the linking thread would be irrelevant
to the remainder of the configuration. The operation requires additional
runtime syntax and thus complicates the metatheory.

With
\LabTirName
TC-Mix

The above issues are symptomatic of the fact that the process structure after a
link takes place is a forest rather than a tree. However, with

\LabTirName
TC-Mix, we can refine the type schema for
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{link}}
to

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{({S}\mathbin{\times}{\overline{S}})}\mathbin{\multimap}{\mathbf{1}}}
and we can use the following rule:

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(F[\mathbf{link}\;(x,y)]\parallel\phi N)}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}F[()]\parallel\phi N\{y/x^{\prime}\}}

This formulation enables immediate substitution, maximimising
concurrency.
A variant of HGV replacing

E-Reify-Link
and
E-Comm-Link
with
E-Link-Mix
retains
HGV’s metatheory.

Exceptions

In order to support exceptions in the presence of linear
endpoints
[
FLMD19
,
MV18
]
we must have a
way of
cancelling
an endpoint.
Mostrous and Vasconcelos
[
MV18
]
describe a process calculus allowing the

explicit cancellation
of a channel endpoint, accounting for exceptional
scenarios such as a client disconnecting, or a thread encountering an
unrecoverable error. Attempting to communicate with a cancelled endpoint
raises an exception. Fowler
et al.

[
FLMD19
]
extend these
ideas to the functional setting, introducing Exceptional GV (EGV).
EGV supports exceptional behaviour by adding three term-level constructs:

•

a new constant,
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{cancel}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}{S}\mathbin{\multimap}{\mathbf{1}}}
, which
allows us to discard an arbitrary session endpoint with type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}

•

a
construct
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{raise}}
, which raises an exception

•

an exception handling construct
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{try}\;L\;\mathbf{as}\;x\;\mathbf{in}\;M\;\mathbf{otherwise}\;N}

in the style of Benton & Kennedy
[
BK01
]
, which attempts
possibly-failing computation
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}L}
, binding the result to
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
in success
continuation
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
if successful and evaluating
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}
if an exception is raised

Cancellation generates a
zapper thread
(
{\rm ZAp}{x}
) which severs a tree topology
into a forest as in the following example.

\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(\nu yy^{\prime})(\circ x^{\prime}\parallel\circ y^{\prime}\parallel\bullet(\mathbf{cancel}\;{x};\mathbf{wait}\;{y}))

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet\medspace(\mathbf{cancel}\;x;\mathbf{wait}\;y)}

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\medspace x^{\prime}}

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\medspace y^{\prime}}

\longrightarrow

\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}(\nu xx^{\prime})(\nu yy^{\prime})(\circ x^{\prime}\parallel\circ y^{\prime}\parallel{\rm ZAp}{x}\parallel\bullet(();\mathbf{wait}\;{y})

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\rm ZAp}{x}}

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\medspace x^{\prime}}

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\bullet\medspace(();\mathbf{wait}\;y)}

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\circ\medspace y^{\prime}}

The configuration on the left has a tree process structure. However, after
reduction, we obtain the configuration on the right which is clearly a forest
and thus needs
\LabTirName
TC-Mix to be typeable.
We have described a
synchronous
version of EGV, but extending our
treatment to asynchrony as in the work of
[
FLMD19
]
is a routine
adaptation.

7.
Can we separate fork?

Hyper-environments allow us to cleanly separate name restriction and parallel
composition in process configurations. A natural follow-on question is whether
we could use the same technique at the level of
terms
in order to
split
{{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{fork}}}
into separate constructs for creating a channel
and spawning a process. As tantalising a prospect this is, we argue that
the disadvantages outweigh the benefits.

Suppose we were to extend term typing to allow hyper-environments,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}
,and were to introduce terms

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle=\mathbf{new}\;\mathbf{in}\;M}
to create a channel and

{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;\langle\rangle=\mathbf{spawn}\;M\;\mathbf{in}\;N}
to spawn a thread, with the following typing rules:

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}S}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\overline{S}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\Gamma_{2}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\mathbf{let}\;\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle=\mathbf{new}\;\mathbf{in}\;M}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}\quad\textsc{TM-LetNew}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathbf{end}_{!}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{G}}\parallel{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}\mathcal{H}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}{\mathbf{let}\;\langle\rangle=\mathbf{spawn}\;M\;\mathbf{in}\;N}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.4766}T}}\quad\textsc{TM-LetSpawn}

These rather ad-hoc rules mirror hypersequent cut and hypersequent composition:
\LabTirName
TM-LetNew creates a new channel with endpoints
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}
, and requires them to be used in separate threads in the continuation
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
; and
\LabTirName
TM-LetSpawn takes a term
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
, spawns it as a child thread, and continues as
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}
. Using these rules, we can encode
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{fork}\;M}
as
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle=\mathbf{new}\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{let}\;\langle\rangle=\mathbf{spawn}\;(M\;x)\;\mathbf{in}\;x^{\prime}}
.

Where else can we allow hyper-environments? In HCP, we have two options: (1) if
we restrict
all logical rules
to singleton hypersequents and allow
hyper-environments only in the rules for name restriction and parallel
composition, we can use standard sequential
semantics
[
MP18
,
KMP19b
]
; but (2) if we allow
hyper-environments in
any logical rules
, we must use a semantics which
allows the corresponding actions to be delayed
[
KMP19a
]
.
This is unlikely to be a property of logical rules, but rather due to the fact
that the logical rules correspond exactly to the communication actions—which
block reduction—and the structural rules to name restriction and parallel
composition—which do not. Therefore, we expect the positions where
hypersequents can safely occur to follow from the structure of evaluation
contexts and whether any blocking term perform a communication action.

Regardless of our choice, we would be left with restrictions on the syntax of
terms that seem sensible in a process calculus, but are surprising in a
\lambda
-calculus. In the strictest variant, where we disallow hyper-environments in all but the above two rules, uses of
\LabTirName
TM-LetNew and
\LabTirName
TM-LetSpawn may be interleaved, but no other construct may appear between a
\LabTirName
TM-LetNew and its corresponding
\LabTirName
TM-LetSpawn. Consider the following terms, where
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}M}
uses
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}y}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}N}
uses
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}x^{\prime}}
. Term (
1
) may be well-typed, but (
2
) is always ill-typed:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;y=1\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{let}\;\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle=\mathbf{new}\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{let}\;\langle\rangle=\mathbf{spawn}\;M\;\mathbf{in}\;N}

(1)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle=\mathbf{new}\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{let}\;y=1\;\mathbf{in}\;\mathbf{let}\;\langle\rangle=\mathbf{spawn}\;M\;\mathbf{in}\;N}

(2)

Note that
{\color[rgb]{0.6445,0,0.2188}\mathbf{let}\;\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle=\mathbf{new}\;\mathbf{in}\;M}
is a single, monolithic term
constructor—exactly what hypersequents were meant to prevent! However, if we
attempt to decompose these constructors, we find that these are not the regular
product and unit types.

8.
Related work

Session Types and Functional Languages

Session types were originally introduced in the context of process
calculi
[
Hon93
,
THK94
,
HVK98
]
, however they have been vastly integrated also in functional calculi, a line of work initiated by Gay and collaborators
[
VRG04
,
VGR06
,
GV10
]
.
This family of calculi builds session types directly into a lambda
calculus.
Toninho
et al.

[
TCP13
]
take an alternative approach,
stratifying their system into a session-typed process calculus and a
separate functional calculus.
There are many pragmatic embeddings of session type systems in
existing functional programming
languages
[
NT04
,
PT08
,
SE08
,
IYA10
,
OY16
,
KD21a
]
. A
detailed survey is given by Orchard and Yoshida

[
OY17
]
.

Propositions as Sessions

When Girard introduced linear logic
[
Gir87
]
he suggested a
connection with concurrency.
Abramsky
[
Abr94
]
and Bellin and Scott
[
BS94
]
give
embeddings of linear logic proofs in
\pi
-calculus, where cut
reduction is simulated by
\pi
-calculus reduction.
Both embeddings interpret tensor as parallel composition. The
